Or I'll start thinking about the robotics kids I advise and worrying that they're falling behind. \n\nAnd then I just end up worrying that I'm disappointing my wife and my family because I can't seem to just keep everything under control. So, yeah, it really just spreads to everything.\n\nDoctor: Over the past six months, has this worrying happened on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean... looking back over the last six or seven months, it really is almost every day. There might be a day here or there where I'm just too busy with the students to notice it as much, but for the most part, yeah, it's a daily thing. It just doesn't really turn off.\n\nDoctor: When you try to stop or control the worrying, how difficult is that for you?\n\nPatient: It's... it's very difficult. I try, I really do. I can tell myself to stop, to just focus on the class I'm teaching right in front of me. But it just takes one small thought—like remembering a late invoice for the house, or noticing a student who's falling behind—and it just sets off this whole chain of what-ifs. Once that starts, I just can't seem to shut it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Actually... no, not really. Sleep is probably the one thing that hasn't been too bad. By the time I finish grading papers and finally get to bed, I'm usually just so exhausted from the day that I fall right asleep. So, I haven't really noticed any issues there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Sleep is probably the one thing that hasn't been too bad. By the time I finish grading papers and finally get to bed, I'm usually just so exhausted from the day that I fall right asleep. So, I haven't really noticed any issues there.\n\nDoctor: Has the remodeling from the pipe burst been completed yet?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't. I'm... well, I'm actually not entirely sure when it's going to be finished. They're still working on things, so it's just kind of up in the air right now.\n\nDoctor: If the remodeling were completely finished tomorrow, do you think your excessive worrying would still continue about other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I... honestly, I'm not sure. I mean, the house is a huge source of stress right now, but... I don't really know if the worrying would just go away, or if my mind would just latch onto something else at school. I guess I just don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Before the pipe burst and remodeling began, were you already experiencing excessive worry like this about several areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It... it pretty much all started when the pipe burst. That was about seven months ago. Once the remodeling began and just threw the whole house into chaos, that's when this constant worrying really kicked into gear. So, no, it wasn't like this before then.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty concentrating because of the worrying?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I'm still able to focus on my lesson plans and grade my students' papers just fine. The worrying is definitely on my mind a lot, but... it hasn't really gotten in the way of my ability to concentrate on what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: How much has this worry interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely getting in the way more than I'd like. I know I just said I can concentrate fine when I actually sit down to work, but... finding the time and energy to do it is getting harder. I've actually started falling behind on my lesson prep and grading. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's taking a toll, too. I usually manage some of the behind-the-scenes work for our local community theater, but I had to give parts of that up recently. I just couldn't keep up with all of it anymore with this constantly weighing on me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It feels like I'm constantly bracing for bad news, even when I'm just sitting at my desk or trying to watch TV at night. It just doesn't seem to let up.\n\nDoctor: Did this tension begin after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it did. This has been going on for about seven months now. We had a pipe burst at the house, and... well, the remodeling that came after just threw everything into chaos. That's when I really noticed it starting.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry mainly about the house repairs, or does it also extend to many other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: It definitely extends to other parts of my life now. It started with the house, but... well, now my mind just keeps jumping around. I'll be worrying about whether the contractors missed something in the walls, and then suddenly I'm stressing over school, wondering if my lesson plans are slipping. Or I'll start thinking about the robotics kids I advise and worrying that they're falling behind. \n\nAnd then I just end up worrying that I'm disappointing my wife and my family because I can't seem to just keep everything under control. So, yeah, it really just spreads to everything.\n\nDoctor: Over the past six months, has this worrying happened on most days?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean... looking back over the last six or seven months, it really is almost every day. There might be a day here or there where I'm just too busy with the students to notice it as much, but for the most part, yeah, it's a daily thing. It just doesn't really turn off.\n\nDoctor: When you try to stop or control the worrying, how difficult is that for you?\n\nPatient: It's... it's very difficult. I try, I really do. I can tell myself to stop, to just focus on the class I'm teaching right in front of me. But it just takes one small thought—like remembering a late invoice for the house, or noticing a student who's falling behind—and it just sets off this whole chain of what-ifs. Once that starts, I just can't seem to shut it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Actually... no, not really. Yeah, I have. It's been pretty constant lately. By the time the last bell rings at school, I just feel completely wrung out. I can tell myself to stop, to just focus on the class I'm teaching right in front of me. But it just takes one small thought—like remembering a late invoice for the house, or noticing a student who's falling behind—and it just sets off this whole chain of what-ifs. Once that starts, I just can't seem to shut it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Actually... no, not really. Sleep is probably the one thing that hasn't been too bad. By the time I finish grading papers and finally get to bed, I'm usually just so exhausted from the day that I fall right asleep. So, I haven't really noticed any issues there.\n\nDoctor: Has the remodeling from the pipe burst been completed yet?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't. I'm... well, I'm actually not entirely sure when it's going to be finished. They're still working on things, so it's just kind of up in the air right now.\n\nDoctor: If the remodeling were completely finished tomorrow, do you think your excessive worrying would still continue about other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I... honestly, I'm not sure. I mean, the house is a huge source of stress right now, but... I don't really know if the worrying would just go away, or if my mind would just latch onto something else at school. I guess I just don't know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Before the pipe burst and remodeling began, were you already experiencing excessive worry like this about several areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. It... it pretty much all started when the pipe burst. That was about seven months ago. Once the remodeling began and just threw the whole house into chaos, that's when this constant worrying really kicked into gear. So, no, it wasn't like this before then.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty concentrating because of the worrying?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I'm still able to focus on my lesson plans and grade my students' papers just fine. The worrying is definitely on my mind a lot, but... it hasn't really gotten in the way of my ability to concentrate on what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: How much has this worry interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely getting in the way more than I'd like. I know I just said I can concentrate fine when I actually sit down to work, but... finding the time and energy to do it is getting harder. I've actually started falling behind on my lesson prep and grading. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's taking a toll, too. I usually manage some of the behind-the-scenes work for our local community theater, but I had to give parts of that up recently. I just couldn't keep up with all of it anymore with this constantly weighing on me.\n\nDoctor: On days when you are not dealing with the house repairs or remodeling, do you still spend a substantial amount of time worrying about unrelated areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It doesn't really matter if there's nothing going on with the house that day, the worrying is still there. \n\nMy mind just... it keeps jumping around. I'll start off worrying about whether the contractors missed something in the house, and then that turns into worrying about whether my lesson plans are slipping. Then I'm stressing over the robotics kids at school, wondering if they're falling behind because I'm distracted. And... well, then I just end up worrying that I'm disappointing my wife and the rest of the family because I'm not keeping everything under control. It just constantly shifts from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or unable to relax, even when you have a chance to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I feel pretty wound up most of the day, to be honest. It's like I'm just waiting for something else to go wrong. \n\nEven when I have a break and could just sit at my desk, I can't really relax. I'll catch myself pacing around the classroom between periods, or... I'm constantly checking my phone to see if there are any new messages from the contractor. It's just really hard to settle down and actually sit still.\n\nDoctor: Compared with the actual problems caused by the pipe burst and remodeling, how excessive or disproportionate does your worry feel to you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I mean... the pipe burst was a huge mess, so the problems are very real. It's hard for me to step back and judge if my worrying is out of proportion to all of it. \n\nTo me, it just feels like... well, it feels like I have a lot to be concerned about right now. I haven't really thought about whether it's excessive compared to the actual damage. It just feels like what I have to deal with.\n\nDoctor: During the past six months, have you often felt irritable or easily annoyed?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say so. I mean, I'm definitely stressed and wound up, like I mentioned, but I don't think I've been snapping at people or feeling unusually irritable. I'm just mostly anxious and having a hard time relaxing, rather than feeling annoyed with anyone.\n\nDoctor: During the past six months, have you often felt unusually tired or fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It's been pretty constant lately. By the time the last bell rings at school, I just feel completely wrung out. And it's a kind of tired that a decent night's sleep or even a quiet Sunday doesn't really seem to fix. It's gotten to the point where even just sitting down to grade a short stack of quizzes feels like I'm pushing through mud. I'm just drained.\n\nDoctor: Looking at all the different things you worry about, do you feel the amount of worry is much greater than what the actual circumstances would reasonably call for?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess that's sort of like what you asked before. I just don't really have a good way to measure it. The things I worry about, like the house and school, they're real problems. So to me, the worry just feels like what happens when you have a lot on your plate. I can't really tell if it's way out of proportion or not, it's just... it's just there all the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

The worrying is definitely on my mind a lot, but... it hasn't really gotten in the way of my ability to concentrate on what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: How much has this worry interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely getting in the way more than I'd like. I know I just said I can concentrate fine when I actually sit down to work, but... finding the time and energy to do it is getting harder. I've actually started falling behind on my lesson prep and grading. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's taking a toll, too. I usually manage some of the behind-the-scenes work for our local community theater, but I had to give parts of that up recently. I just couldn't keep up with all of it anymore with this constantly weighing on me.\n\nDoctor: On days when you are not dealing with the house repairs or remodeling, do you still spend a substantial amount of time worrying about unrelated areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It doesn't really matter if there's nothing going on with the house that day, the worrying is still there. \n\nMy mind just... it keeps jumping around. Then I'm stressing over the robotics kids at school, wondering if they're falling behind because I'm distracted. The worrying is definitely on my mind a lot, but... it hasn't really gotten in the way of my ability to concentrate on what I need to do.\n\nDoctor: How much has this worry interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely getting in the way more than I'd like. I know I just said I can concentrate fine when I actually sit down to work, but... finding the time and energy to do it is getting harder. I've actually started falling behind on my lesson prep and grading. \n\nAnd outside of school, it's taking a toll, too. I usually manage some of the behind-the-scenes work for our local community theater, but I had to give parts of that up recently. I just couldn't keep up with all of it anymore with this constantly weighing on me.\n\nDoctor: On days when you are not dealing with the house repairs or remodeling, do you still spend a substantial amount of time worrying about unrelated areas of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It doesn't really matter if there's nothing going on with the house that day, the worrying is still there. \n\nMy mind just... it keeps jumping around. I'll start off worrying about whether the contractors missed something in the house, and then that turns into worrying about whether my lesson plans are slipping. Then I'm stressing over the robotics kids at school, wondering if they're falling behind because I'm distracted. And... well, then I just end up worrying that I'm disappointing my wife and the rest of the family because I'm not keeping everything under control. It just constantly shifts from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or unable to relax, even when you have a chance to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I feel pretty wound up most of the day, to be honest. It's like I'm just waiting for something else to go wrong. \n\nEven when I have a break and could just sit at my desk, I can't really relax. I'll catch myself pacing around the classroom between periods, or... I'm constantly checking my phone to see if there are any new messages from the contractor. It's just really hard to settle down and actually sit still.\n\nDoctor: Compared with the actual problems caused by the pipe burst and remodeling, how excessive or disproportionate does your worry feel to you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that. I mean... the pipe burst was a huge mess, so the problems are very real. It's hard for me to step back and judge if my worrying is out of proportion to all of it. \n\nTo me, it just feels like... well, it feels like I have a lot to be concerned about right now. I haven't really thought about whether it's excessive compared to the actual damage. It just feels like what I have to deal with.\n\nDoctor: During the past six months, have you often felt irritable or easily annoyed?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't really say so. I mean, I'm definitely stressed and wound up, like I mentioned, but I don't think I've been snapping at people or feeling unusually irritable. I'm just mostly anxious and having a hard time relaxing, rather than feeling annoyed with anyone.\n\nDoctor: During the past six months, have you often felt unusually tired or fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It's been pretty constant lately. By the time the last bell rings at school, I just feel completely wrung out. And it's a kind of tired that a decent night's sleep or even a quiet Sunday doesn't really seem to fix. It's gotten to the point where even just sitting down to grade a short stack of quizzes feels like I'm pushing through mud. I'm just drained.\n\nDoctor: Looking at all the different things you worry about, do you feel the amount of worry is much greater than what the actual circumstances would reasonably call for?\n\nPatient: I don't know... I guess that's sort of like what you asked before. I just don't really have a good way to measure it. The things I worry about, like the house and school, they're real problems. So to me, the worry just feels like what happens when you have a lot on your plate. I can't really tell if it's way out of proportion or not, it's just... it's just there all the time.\n\nDoctor: When things at school and home are going well, do you still find yourself worrying excessively about other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, if by some miracle everything at school is actually calm, and the house is fine, I usually feel okay. I don't really go looking for other random things to stress about. \n\nIt's just that those main things take up so much of my mental space, so it's pretty rare that they're both completely fine at the exact same time. But no, if things are actually good there, I'm not really worrying about other areas of my life.\n\nDoctor: When there are no specific problems happening at home or school, do you still worry about hypothetical problems that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, if the current stuff is actually quiet, I don't sit around inventing things that might go wrong down the road. The day-to-day realities of teaching and keeping up with the house are usually more than enough. So, if there are no actual problems happening right then, I'm not usually stressing about hypothetical ones.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
